M\times\mathbb{R}^{s}
, for some
s\geq 1
.

(ii)

Let
\Sigma
be a
(
smooth, PL
)
structure on
W
.

(iii)

Let
C\subseteq M\times\{0\}
be a closed subset such that there is a neighbourhood
N(C)
of
C\subseteq W
on which the
(
smooth, PL
)
structure
\Sigma
is a product
\Sigma|_{N(C)}=\sigma\times\mathbb{R}^{s}
for some
(
smooth, PL
)
structure
\sigma
on
N(C)
. If
n=5
then suppose that
\partial M\subseteq C
.

(iv)

Let
D\subseteq M\times\{0\}
be another closed subset.

(v)

Let
V\subseteq W
be an open neighbourhood of
D\setminus C
in
M\times\mathbb{R}^{s}
.

Then we have the following.

(1)

A
(
smooth, PL
)
structure
\Sigma^{\prime}
on
W
that equals
\Sigma
on
(W\setminus V)\cup((C\times\mathbb{R}^{s})\cap W)
and is a product
(
smooth, PL
)
structure
\rho\times\mathbb{R}^{s}
on
(N(D)\times\mathbb{R}^{s})\cap W
for some neighbourhood
N(D)
of
D
and for some
(
smooth, PL
)
structure
\rho
on
N(D)
.

(2)

A concordance of
(
smooth, PL
)
structures from
\Sigma
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
, that is a product concordance on some neighbourhood of
(W\setminus V)\cup((C\times\mathbb{R}^{s})\cap W)
and that is a product near
W\times\{i\}
for
i=0,1
.

Note that the Concordance implies Isotopy Theorem
[
KS77
, Essay I, Theorem 4.1, p. 25]
means that the concordances in Theorems
4.16
and
4.18
can be upgraded to isotopies of (smooth, PL) structures under the same hypotheses on dimensions, that is if
n\geq 6

or
if
n=5
and the structures already agree on
\partial M
.

We start with a structure on
W
that is a product structure over
C
. We obtain a concordance of structures to a product structure on
D
, supported in
V
, and that is a product concordance over
C
. We therefore have a product structure in some neighbourhood of
C\cup D
.

Chapter 5
Tubular neighbourhoods

Every smooth submanifold of a smooth manifold admits a normal vector bundle and, by the smooth Tubular Neighbourhood Theorem, also admits a tubular
neighbourhood
[
Hir94
, Sections 5 & 6]
,
[
Wal16
, Chapter 2.5]
. However, in the topological category submanifolds may not admit normal vector bundles, a general problem we discuss further below and in Chapter
6
once we have developed the necessary language. Curiously, in the special case of
4
-manifolds these general problems do not exist, and familiar smooth results hold true using an appropriate notion of normal vector bundles (Definition
5.12
).

5.1.
Tubular neighbourhoods: existence and uniqueness

In the literature one can find many different definitions of tubular neighbourhoods for smooth submanifolds. We will give a definition for manifolds that is modelled on the definition provided by Wall
[
Wal16
]
for smooth manifolds. To do so we first need one extra definition.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional manifold. We say a subset
W\subseteq M
is a
k
-dimensional submanifold with corners
if given any
p\in W
there exists a chart of the type (1), (2) or (3) as in Definition
2.5
above,
or if

(4)

there exists a chart
\Phi\colon U\to V
of type (ii) for
M
such that

\Phi(U\cap W)\,\,\subseteq\,\,\{(0,\dots,0,x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\mid x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\mbox{ with }x_{k-1}\geq 0\mbox{ and }x_{k}\geq 0\}

and with
\Phi(p)\in\{(0,\dots,0,x_{1},\dots,x_{k-2},0,0)\mid x_{1},\dots,x_{k-2}\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

If
W
is an
n
-dimensional submanifold with corners we write

\partial_{0}W\,\,:=\,\,W\cap\overline{M\setminus W},\quad\partial_{1}W\,\,:=\,\,W\cap\partial M,

and we note that

\operatorname{Int}W\,\,=\,\,W\setminus\partial_{0}W.

Figure 5.1.
Definition of
\partial_{0}
and
\partial_{1}W
of a submanifold.

Remark 5.2
.

The complement of the interior of a codimension 0 submanifold with corners is a submanifold with corners, with the obvious corners.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
M
be an
n
-manifold and let
X
be a compact, proper,
k
-dimensional submanifold.
A
tubular neighbourhood
for
X
is a pair
(N,p\colon N\to X)
with the following properties:

(1)

N
is a neighbourhood of
X
;

(2)

N
is a codimension zero submanifold with corners of
M
;

(3)

the map
p\colon N\to X
is a linear
D^{n-k}
-bundle such that
p(x)=x
for all
x\in X
;

(4)

\partial_{1}N=p^{-1}(\partial X)
.

Here linear means that there exists an atlas of local trivialisations such that the transition maps take values in
\operatorname{O}(n-k)
instead of
\operatorname{Homeo}(D^{n-k})
.

Remark 5.4
.

In the topological category, tubular neighbourhoods do not always exist. Indeed it is shown in
[
Hir68
, Theorem 4]
that there exists a
4
-dimensional submanifold of
S^{7}
that does not admit a tubular neighbourhood.

Fortunately, for submanifolds of
4
-manifolds, tubular neighbourhoods exist and they are unique in the appropriate sense.

Theorem 5.5
.

(Tubular Neighbourhood Theorem)

Every compact proper submanifold
X
of a
4
-manifold
M
admits a tubular neighbourhood.

Theorem 5.6
.

(Uniqueness of tubular neighbourhoods)

Let
M
be a
4
-manifold and let
X
be a compact proper
k
-dimensional submanifold. Furthermore let
p_{i}\colon N_{i}\to X
,
i=1,2
be two tubular neighbourhoods of
X
, with inclusion maps
\iota_{i}\colon N_{i}\to M
.
Then there exists an isomorphism
\Psi\colon N_{1}\to N_{2}
of linear disc bundles such that

\iota_{2}\circ\Psi\colon N_{1}\to M
and
\iota_{1}\colon N_{2}\to M
are ambiently isotopic rel.
X
.

The proofs of the above two theorems rely on the existence and uniqueness results for normal vector bundles in
[
FQ90
, Section 9]
, which we discuss further in Section
5.2
. Thus we postpone the proofs of the Theorems
5.5
and
5.6
to Section
5.3
.
Right now, let us first observe some nice consequences of the existence and uniqueness of tubular neighbourhoods.

Remark 5.7
.

Let
X
be a compact proper submanifold of a
4
-manifold
M
.
By Theorem
5.5
we can pick a tubular neighbourhood
p\colon N\to X
. We refer to
E_{X}:=M\setminus\operatorname{Int}N
as the
exterior of
X
. Note
E_{X}\subseteq M
is a submanifold with corners; cf. Remark
5.2
.
By Theorem
5.6
the homeomorphism type of the exterior is well-defined.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
X
be a compact proper submanifold of a
4
-manifold
M
.
The exterior
E_{X}
of
X
is a deformation retract of the complement
M\setminus X
.

Proof.

Let
p\colon N\to X
be a tubular neighbourhood for
X
. Using the fact that
p
is a
linear
bundle, introduce compatible radial coordinates in the fibres and isotope
N\setminus X
radially outwards.
This implies that
\partial_{0}N
is a deformation retract of
N\setminus X
. But this also implies that the exterior
E_{X}=M\setminus N
is a deformation retract of
M\setminus X
.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
X
be a proper submanifold of a compact
4
-manifold
M
.
If
X
is compact, then the fundamental group of each component of
M\setminus X
is finitely generated, and
H_{*}(M\setminus X)
is finitely generated.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
5.8
that
M\setminus X
is homotopy equivalent to the exterior
E_{X}
of
X
. Each connected component of
E_{X}
is a compact 4-manifold since we assume that
M
is compact. The corollary is now a consequence of Corollary
3.18
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
X\subseteq M
be a compact, proper
2
-dimensional orientable submanifold of a compact, orientable
4
-manifold
M
, such that each connected component of
X
has nonempty boundary. Then the tubular neighbourhood of Theorem
5.5
is homeomorphic to
X\times D^{2}
.

Proof.

Connected surfaces with nonempty boundary are homotopy equivalent to wedges of circles. Every orientable linear
D^{n}
-bundle over a space
X
is classified up to isomorphism by a homotopy class of maps
X\to\operatorname{BSO}(n)
. As
\operatorname{SO}(n)
is connected, so
\operatorname{BSO}(n)
is simply connected, and any orientable linear disc bundle over
X
is trivial. In particular, this is true when
n=2
.
∎

5.2.
Normal vector bundles

The reader will be familiar with the definition of a normal vector bundle when working in the smooth category: if
X\subseteq M
is a smooth submanifold of a smooth manifold, then the normal vector bundle is defined as the quotient of the vector bundle
TM|_{X}
by the subbundle
TX
. This definition uses the smooth structure to ensure the existence of tangent vector bundles, and vector bundles are a strong enough bundle technology to ensure the existence
of quotient bundles. While some (weaker) canonical tangential structures do exist in the topological category (see Chapter
6
), the idea of a ‘quotient bundle’ no longer makes sense for them.

In the topological category, following
[
FQ90
, Section 9]
, we will use a definition of normal vector bundle that is much closer to the geometry of tubular neighbourhoods. We begin with a definition that is almost what we need but suffers from a slight technical problem, which we then remedy.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
M
be an
n
-manifold and let
X
be a proper
k
-dimensional submanifold. An
internal linear bundle over
X
is a pair
(E,p\colon E\to X)
with the following properties.

(1)

E
is a neighbourhood of
X
;

(2)

E
is a codimension zero submanifold of
M
;

(3)

the map
p\colon E\to X
is an
(n-k)
-dimensional vector bundle such that
p(x)=x
for all
x\in X
;

(4)

\partial E=p^{-1}(\partial X)
.

An internal linear bundle
(E,p\colon E\to X)
is intended to mirror the notion, from the smooth category, of an
open
tubular neighbourhood of
X
. As such, the definition as stands suffers from the potential technical problem that the closure of
E
in
M
, which should be a closed tubular neighbourhood, may no longer be a submanifold; see Figure
5.2
. As in
[
FQ90
, p. 137]
, we use the following additional idea to rule out this problem.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
M
be an
n
-manifold, let
X
be a proper
k
-dimensional submanifold, and let
(E,p\colon E\to X)
be an internal linear bundle over
X
. Suppose that given any
(n-k)
-dimensional vector bundle
(F,q\colon F\to X)
, any radial homeomorphism from an open convex disc bundle of
F
to
E
can be extended to a homeomorphism from the whole of
F
to a neighbourhood of
E
. Then we say
(E,p\colon E\to X)
is
extendable
.

Figure 5.2.
Non-extendable internal linear bundle.

Now we can define the notion of a normal vector bundle.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
M
be a
n
-manifold and let
X
be a proper
k
-dimensional submanifold. A
normal vector bundle
for
X
is an internal linear bundle that is extendable.
(Note that the same concept is called a
normal bundle
on

[
FQ90
, p. 137]
, we prefer the name normal
vector
bundle.)

Theorem 5.14
.

(Existence of normal vector bundles)

Every proper submanifold of a compact 4-manifold admits a normal vector bundle.

Remark 5.15
.

Generally, in high dimensions, the existence of normal vector bundles is peculiar to when the submanifold has low dimension or low codimension. We refer the reader to
[
FQ90
, Section 9.4]
for a discussion of the other known situations where these objects always exist. Here is a summary of the known cases.
A submanifold of dimension at most 3 in a closed manifold of dimension at least 5 has a normal vector bundle
[
FQ90
, p. 150]
. Codimension one submanifolds have normal vector bundles
[
Bro62
, Theorem 3]
. That every codimension two submanifold of a manifold of dimension not equal to four has a normal vector bundles was shown in
[
KS75
]
, and this was extended to include dimension four in
[
FQ90
, Section 9.3]
.
It is striking that, while among smooth manifolds dimension 4 exhibits worse than usual behaviour, in the topological category the existence of normal vector bundles seems to show that in this respect it is among the better behaved of the dimensions.

For the proof of Theorem
5.14
we will essentially appeal to results of
[
FQ90
]
. We reproduce these results here for the benefit of the reader.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
N
be a proper submanifold of a
4
-manifold
M
, with a closed subset
\partial N\subseteq K\subseteq N
and a normal vector bundle over some neighbourhood
U
of
K
in
N
. Then there is a normal vector bundle over
N
that agrees with the given one over some neighbourhood
V\subseteq U
of
K
. Moreover this extension is unique up to ambient isotopy relative to some neighbourhood
W\subseteq V
of
K
.

Proof of Theorem
5.16
.

The existence statement of the theorem follows immediately from
[
FQ90
, Theorem 9.3A]
and
the uniqueness statement of the theorem follows immediately from
[
FQ90
, Theorem 9.3D]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.14
.

Let
X
be a proper submanifold of a compact 4-manifold
M
.
The case that
X
has no boundary follows immediately from
the existence statement of Theorem
5.16
with
K=\varnothing
.
The case that
X
has nonempty boundary follows also from Theorem
5.16
if we apply more care. We sketch the argument.

First it is well-known that
given any pair
(X,\Sigma)
where
X
is a 3-manifold and
\Sigma
is a proper submanifold,
then there exists a smooth structure on
X
such that
\Sigma
is a smooth submanifold.
Even though this fact is well-known and often used, it is hard to give complete references.
The existence of a smooth structure on
X
follows from
[
Moi77
, p. 252 and 253]
and
[
Mun60b
,
Theorems 6.2 and 6.3]
. The extra complication of having a proper submanifold is
partly taken care of by

[
Bin83
, Theorem XVIII.4.B]
.
Thus we can view the submanifold
\partial X\subseteq\partial M
as a smooth submanifold. Hence it has a smooth normal vector bundle, see e.g.
[
Kos93
, Chapter III.2]
or
[
Lan02b
, Section IV.5]
.

Next use the Collar Neighbourhood Theorem
2.21
to obtain a collar
\partial M\times[0,1]\subseteq M
that restricts to a collar
\partial X\times[0,1]
for the boundary of
X
. Extend the smooth tubular neighbourhood of
\partial X\subseteq\partial M
into the collar by taking a product with
[0,1]
.

Finally, consider the 4-manifold without boundary
M^{\prime}:=M\setminus(\partial M\times[0,\frac{1}{2}])
. What remains of
X
is a submanifold
N:=X\setminus(\partial X\times[0,\frac{1}{2}])
.
The submanifold
N
already has a preferred normal vector bundle on the closed subset

K:=\partial X\times(1/2,1]
.
Now apply Theorem
5.16
to the triple
(M^{\prime},N,K)
to obtain a normal vector bundle
E\to N
agreeing with the given one on
K
. The normal vector bundles over
N
and
\partial X\times[0,1]
agree on the overlap
K
. Thus they define a normal vector bundle on all of
X
.
∎

Figure 5.3.
Illustration of the proof of Theorem
5.14
.

Next we turn to the uniqueness of normal vector bundles.

Theorem 5.17
.

(Uniqueness of normal vector bundles)

Let
M
be a compact 4-manifold and let
X
be a proper submanifold of
M
.
Suppose we are given two normal vector bundles
p_{i}\colon E_{i}\to X
,
i=1,2
over
X
.
For
i=1,2
let
\iota_{i}\colon E_{i}\to M
be the inclusion map.
Then there exists a bundle isomorphism
f\colon E_{1}\xrightarrow{\cong}E_{2}
such that
\iota_{2}\circ f
and
\iota_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel.
X
.

Proof.

If
X
has no boundary, then the theorem is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.16
.
Now suppose that
X
has nonempty boundary.

First we claim that any normal vector bundle of
X
is obtained by the construction outlined in the proof of Theorem
5.14
. To see this, let
p\colon E\to X
be a normal vector bundle. Pick a collar neighbourhood
\partial X\times[0,2]\subseteq X
. Since
p
is extendable, we can view
p
as the interior of a disc bundle
q\colon F\to X
in
M
. Write
C:=q^{-1}(\partial X)\subseteq\partial M
. The disc bundle
q\colon q^{-1}(\partial X\times[0,2])\to\partial X\times[0,2]
defines a collar neighbourhood
C\times[0,2]
for the compact submanifold
C
of
\partial M
. By the Collar Neighbourhood Theorem
2.16
we can extend the collar neighbourhood
C\times[0,1]
of
C
to a collar neighbourhood
\partial M\times[0,1]
. With this choice of collar neighbourhood of
\partial M
, the construction in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, with further appropriate choices, gives rise to the normal vector bundle
p\colon E\to X
. This completes the proof of the claim.

After this long preamble it suffices to prove the theorem for any two normal vector bundles obtained as in the proof of Theorem
5.14
.
Uniqueness follows by arguing that each step in the proof of existence of normal vector bundles was essentially unique. The proofs of uniqueness in the three steps make use of the following ingredients.

First, apply the uniqueness statement for normal vector bundles of submanifolds of smooth manifolds to
\partial X\subseteq\partial M
e.g.
[
Kos93
, Chapter III.2]
or
[
Lan02b
, Section IV.5]
.

Next use the uniqueness of collar neighbourhoods as formulated in Theorem
2.18
, applied to the two collar neighbourhoods of
\partial M
subordinate to the given normal vector bundles of
X
.

Finally apply the full relative version of Theorem
5.16
to extend the normal vector bundle uniquely over the rest of
X
.
∎

5.3.
Tubular neighbourhoods: proofs

Now we will use the results from the previous section to prove
Theorems
5.5

and
5.6
, i.e. we will prove the existence and uniqueness of tubular neighbourhoods.
First we show how one can obtain tubular neighbourhoods from normal vector bundles.

Definition 5.18
.

(1)

A
form
over a real vector space
V
is an
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear symmetric map
g\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
. It is called
positive definite
if for every
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
we have
g(v,v)>0
.

(2)

Let
p\colon E\to X
be a vector bundle over a topological space
X
. Given
x\in X
, write
E_{x}:=p^{-1}(x)
.
A
positive definite form

g=\{g_{x}\}_{x\in X}
consists of a positive definite form
g_{x}
over every
E_{x}
such that
g_{x}
changes continuously with
x
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
X
be a compact manifold and let
p\colon E\to X
be an
n
-dimensional vector bundle.
Then the space of positive definite forms on
E
is nonempty and convex.
Furthermore, let
g=\{g_{x}\}_{x\in X}
be a positive definite form on
E
and consider the map

p\colon E(g)\,:=\,\bigcup\limits_{x\in X}{}\{v\in E_{x}\mid g_{x}(v,v)\leq 1\}\,\,\to\,\,X.

This map has the following properties.

(1)

The map
p|_{E(g)}\colon E(g)\to X
is a linear
D^{n}
-bundle.

(2)

Given two different positive definite forms
g
and
h
on
E
there
exists a continuous map
H\colon E\times[0,1]\to E
with the following properties.

(a)

We have
H_{0}=\operatorname{Id}
.

(b)

The map
H_{1}
restricts to an isomorphism
H_{1}\colon E(g)\to E(h)
of linear
D^{n}
-bundles.

Proof.

(1)

This statement can be proved easily using the observation that the set of positive definition
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear symmetric forms on a real vector space is nonempty and convex.

(2)

The proof of this statement follows from the same argument as
[
Wal16
, Lemmas 2.5.2 and 2.5.4]
.∎

Let
X
be a compact manifold and let
p\colon E\to X
be a vector bundle. Given a positive definite form
g
on
E
we refer to

p\colon E(g)\,\,:=\,\,\bigcup\limits_{x\in X}{}\{v\in E_{x}\mid g_{x}(v,v)\leq 1\}\,\,\to\,\,X

as a
corresponding disc bundle
. It follows from Lemma
5.19
that for most purposes the precise choice of
g
is irrelevant.

We can now prove the existence of tubular neighbourhoods.

Proof of the Tubular Neighbourhood Theorem
5.5
.

Let
X
be a compact proper submanifold of a
4
-manifold
M
.
By Theorem
5.14
there exists a normal vector bundle
p\colon N\to X
for
X
.
By Lemma
5.19
(1)
there exists a corresponding disc bundle. Using the uniqueness statement of
Lemma
5.19
(2)
locally one can show that this disc bundle is a submanifold with corner and a tubular neighbourhood.
∎

The uniqueness proof for tubular neighbourhoods also requires us to associate a normal vector bundle to a tubular neighbourhood.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
M
be a compact
4
-manifold and let
X
be a compact proper
k
-dimensional submanifold. Let
p\colon N\to X
be a tubular neighbourhood for
X
. There exists a normal vector bundle
q\colon E\to X
and a positive definite form
g
such that
N=E(g)
and
p\colon N\to X
equals
q\colon E(g)\to X
.

We call
q\colon E\to X
a
corresponding normal vector bundle
.

Proof.

Let
p\colon N\to X
be a tubular neighbourhood for
X
. Recall that we have
\operatorname{Int}N=N\setminus\partial_{0}N
. Consider

W:=M\setminus\operatorname{Int}N
. This is a compact
4
-manifold.
Pick a collar neighbourhood
\partial W\times[0,1]
and set
E:=N\cup(\partial_{0}N\times[0,\frac{1}{2}))
. We have an obvious projection map
q\colon E\to X
turning
q
into a bundle map where the fibre is given by the open
(4-k)
-ball of radius
\frac{3}{2}
. We leave it to the reader to turn
q\colon N\to X
into an internal linear bundle, to show that it is in fact extendable (at this point one has to use that in the definition of
E
we only used “half” of the collar neighbourhood
\partial_{0}N\times[0,1]
), and to equip
N
with a positive definite form
g
such that
N=E(g)
.
∎

We conclude the chapter with the proof of the uniqueness theorem for tubular neighbourhoods.

Proof of Theorem
5.6
.

Let
M
be a
4
-manifold and let
X
be a compact proper
k
-dimensional submanifold. Furthermore let
p_{i}\colon N_{i}\to X
,
i=1,2
be two tubular neighbourhoods of
X
.
For
i=1,2
, let
q_{i}\colon E_{i}\to X
be two corresponding normal vector bundles and let
g_{i}
be the positive definite forms provided by Lemma
5.20
.
It follows from Theorem
5.17
that there exists a bundle isomorphism
f\colon E_{1}\xrightarrow{\cong}E_{2}
such that
\iota_{2}\circ f
and
\iota_{1}
are ambiently isotopic rel.
X
. It follows from the definitions that
N_{2}
is equivalent to the
disc bundle defined by
f^{*}g_{2}
on
E_{1}
.
It follows from the
Lemma
5.19
(2)
together with the
Isotopy Extension Theorem
2.20
that
f^{*}g_{2}
and
g_{1}
define equivalent tubular neighbourhoods. (Strictly speaking we did not formulate the Isotopy Extension Theorem
2.20
for submanifolds with corner, but it is not difficult to prove a generalization.)
∎

Chapter 6
Background on bundle structures

In this chapter we recall the bundle technologies we will need to use in later chapters. The three standard manifold categories smooth (
\operatorname{Diff}
), piecewise linear (
\operatorname{PL}
) and topological (
\operatorname{TOP}
) each have corresponding bundle types, with fibre
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We first discuss the topological groups
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
and
\operatorname{O}(n)
which are structure groups for fibre bundles with fibre
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, corresponding to the
\operatorname{TOP}
and
\operatorname{Diff}
categories. There are natural topologies on
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
and
\operatorname{O}(n)
, so that it is relatively straightforward to discuss the classification of these bundle types. We next discuss the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles that correspond to the
\operatorname{PL}
category. In contrast to
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
and
\operatorname{O}(n)
there is no obvious appropriate topology on the group of piecewise-linear homeomoprhisms of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that fix the origin. For this reason, in the
\operatorname{PL}
category it appears there is no choice but to delve into a more sophisticated approach to classify the bundles of interest, and we approach this via simplicial groups. We finish the section with a discussion of topological
microbundles
.

6.1.
Topological, smooth, and piecewise linear
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles

Before we turn to the different flavours of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles let us first recall some general facts about bundles.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
G
be a topological group
G
. A
universal principal
G
-bundle
is a principal
G
-bundle
p\colon\textup{E}G\to\textup{B}G
such that the following two conditions are satisfied.

(1)

The space
\textup{B}G
is a CW complex.

(2)

Given any principal
G
-bundle
q\colon F\to C
, where
C
is a topological space that is homotopy equivalent to a CW complex,
there exists a map
f\colon C\to\textup{B}G
, unique up to homotopy, such that
q
is isomorphic to the pullback bundle
f^{*}\textup{E}G
.

The base space
\textup{B}G
is called a
classifying space for
G
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Given a topological group
G
, there exists a
universal principal
G
-bundle
p\colon\textup{E}G\to\textup{B}G
.
This principal bundle is unique up to fibre homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

The Milnor join construction
[
Mil56
]
,
[
Hus93
]
or
[
Sta71
]
, or alternatively the geometric bar construction
[
May75
]
, gives an explicit principal
G
-bundle
p\colon E\to B

which has the universal property for all numerable principal
G
-bundles. Since principal
G
-bundles over CW complexes are numerable, the Milnor bundle has the universal property for principal
G
-bundles over CW complexes.

The topological space
B
is not necessarily a CW complex.
Thus we apply the CW approximation theorem to get a CW complex
\widetilde{B}
and a weak homotopy equivalence
\varphi\colon\widetilde{B}\to B
. The pullback bundle
\varphi^{*}E
over
\widetilde{B}
has the desired properties.

The uniqueness of universal principal
G
-bundles over CW complexes follows from a standard argument about universal objects.
∎

Definition 6.3
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
let
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
be the subgroup of homeomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

that fix the origin, topologised using the compact open topology. A principal
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
-bundle
has an associated fibre bundle with fibre
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a preferred 0-section.
Call such a bundle a
topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
.
Let
\operatorname{TOP}
be the colimit
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\scriptscriptstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits\operatorname{TOP}(n)
, in the category of topological groups, under the inclusions

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{TOP}(n)&\to&\operatorname{TOP}(n+1)\\
(f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n})&\mapsto&(f\times\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}).\end{array}

We obtain the corresponding classifying spaces
\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
and
\operatorname{BTOP}
.

Definition 6.4
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
let
\operatorname{O}(n)
be the orthogonal homeomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that fix the origin, topologised in the standard way as a subspace of a vector space

\operatorname{O}(n)\subseteq\operatorname{M}(n\times n,\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}
.
A principal
\operatorname{O}(n)
-bundle
has an associated fibre bundle with fibre
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a preferred 0-section, and such a bundle is in particular a vector bundle.
Define
\operatorname{O}
,
\operatorname{BO}(n)
and
\operatorname{BO}
analogously to Definition
6.3
.

We also introduce the following structure group, for comparison, and use in Chapter
7
.

Definition 6.5
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
let

\operatorname{Diff}(n)
be the subgroup of diffeomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

that fix the origin, topologised using the weak
C^{\infty}
topology
[
Hir94
, § 2.1]
(also called the (weak)
Whitney
topology). A principal
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
-bundle
has an associated fibre bundle with fibre
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a preferred 0-section.
Call such a bundle a
\operatorname{Diff}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
. Define
\operatorname{Diff}
,
\operatorname{BDiff}(n)
and
\operatorname{BDiff}
analogously to Definition
6.3
.

Remark 6.6
.

(1)

The pullback topology on
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
under the inclusion map
\operatorname{Diff}(n)\to\operatorname{TOP}(n)
is by definition the coarsest topology such that
\operatorname{Diff}(n)\to\operatorname{TOP}(n)
is continuous. It equals the compact open topology on the set
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
(equivalent to the weak
C^{0}
topology), not the weak
C^{\infty}
topology. In other words, one does not topologise
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
as a subspace of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
.

(2)

It is nevertheless the case that
\operatorname{Diff}(n)\to\operatorname{TOP}(n)
is a continuous map with respect to the weak
C^{\infty}
topology on
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
and the compact open topology on
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
. There are induced maps
\operatorname{BDiff}(n)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
for each
n
, and
\operatorname{BDiff}\to\operatorname{BTOP}
.

(3)

On the other hand, we now argue that
both
inclusion maps
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{TOP}(n)
and
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{Diff}(n)
induce the standard topology on
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
via pullback (which is a little surprising at first glance). In the former case, simply note that the standard topology on
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
as a vector subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}
is equivalent to the compact open topology, or equivalently the weak
C^{0}
topology. In the latter case, note that as the maps in
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
are linear, and the weak
C^{r}
topologies for
0\leq r<\infty
are defined in terms of partial derivatives
[
Hir94
, § 2.1]
, they are all equivalent topologies on
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. As the weak
C^{\infty}
topology is the limit of the
C^{r}
topologies, this proves the statement.

(4)

The map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{Diff}(n)\times[0,1]&\to&\operatorname{Diff}(n)\\
(f,t)&\mapsto&\left(\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{R}^{n}&\to&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
x&\mapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{t}\cdot f(tx),&\mbox{ if }t\neq 0,\\
\operatorname{D}f_{0}\cdot x\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right)\end{array}

is a deformation retraction from
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
to
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. Finally note that the Gram-Schmidt process can be used to determine a homotopy equivalence
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})\simeq\operatorname{O}(n)
. Thus
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles with structure group
\operatorname{Diff}(n)
always admit a (unique) structure group reduction to ordinary vector bundles whose transition functions lie in
\operatorname{O}(n)
.

The following proposition shows that
\operatorname{BTOP}
and
\operatorname{BDiff}
classify
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles when
\operatorname{CAT}
is
\operatorname{TOP}
or
\operatorname{Diff}
.

Proposition 6.7
.

For
\operatorname{CAT}=\operatorname{TOP}
or
\operatorname{Diff}
, and for each
n\geq 0
, the space
\operatorname{BCAT}(n)
is homotopy equivalent to a CW complex, and there exists a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}}
over
\operatorname{BCAT}(n)
, such that for every
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\xi
over a CW complex, there is a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle map
F\colon\xi\to\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}}
, unique up to homotopy of
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle maps. In particular
F^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}})\cong\xi
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a space homotopy equivalent to a CW complex. There is a 1:1 correspondence between isomorphism classes of
\operatorname{CAT}(n)

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles and isomorphism classes of principal
\operatorname{CAT}(n)
-bundles over
X
. For more details, see e.g.
[
Swi02
, Proposition 11.22]
.

Applying this to the universal principal
\operatorname{CAT}(n)
-bundle of Proposition
6.2
, we obtain a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}}
over
\operatorname{BCAT}(n)
. The desired universal property for
\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}}
is inherited from the universal property of the universal principal
\operatorname{CAT}(n)
-bundle over
\operatorname{BCAT}(n)

∎

We finally move on to the piecewise linear category. For background on piecewise linear topology see
[
RS72
]
.

Definition 6.8
.

A continuous map
f\colon K\to L
between two simplicial complexes
K
and
L
is
piecewise linear
(
\operatorname{PL}
) if there are subdivisions
K^{\prime}
of
K
and
L^{\prime}
of
L
such that
f\colon K^{\prime}\to L^{\prime}
is a simplicial map.

Definition 6.9
.

A
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
is a topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
p\colon E\to B
, where both
E
and
B
are simplicial complexes, where
p\colon E\to B
and the
0
-section are both
\operatorname{PL}
maps, and where for every simplex
\Delta\subseteq B
there exists a
\operatorname{PL}
homeomorphism
\varphi
such that the composition
p^{-1}(\Delta)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Delta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{pr}_{1}}\Delta
is equal to the projection
p
.

The definition of the classifying space for
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles is a little more involved than the constructions for
\operatorname{O}(n)
and
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
, using the technology of semi-simplicial groups. For a gentle introduction to simplicial sets, see
[
Fri12
]
. The canonical reference for classifying spaces constructed using simplicial groups is
[
May75
]
.

Remark 6.10
.

When navigating the various references we use below, the reader should be aware that the terminology for simplicial groups and simplicial sets has changed over the years and there are some clashes between current usage and previous usage. What we are calling a
simplicial set
is, for example, what is defined in
[
Fri12
, Definition 3.2]
. In particular, note that this has both
face
and
degeneracy
maps. This object has historically been called a
complete semi-simplicial set (c.s.s.)
, which has unfortunately in the past been abbreviated to just “semi-simplicial set”. The modern terminology reserves
semi-simplicial set
for a simplicial set without degeneracy maps as part of the data. To add to the confusion, a simplicial set, but without degeneracies, has also historically been called a
\Delta
-set, although in modern terminology this is usually reserved for the semi-simplical version of a simplicial
complex
.

Let
\Delta^{k}
be the standard
k
-simplex. Recall a
simplicial group
is a simplicial object in the category of groups, that is a contravariant functor from the simplicial category to the category of groups
\Delta\to\mathbf{\operatorname{\textbf{Group}}}
.

Definition 6.11
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
let
\operatorname{PL}(n)_{\bullet}
be the simplicial group defined as follows.

(i)

The group
\operatorname{PL}(n)_{k}
assigned to the
k
-simplex is the group of
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle isomorphisms of the trivial bundle
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}
. That is,
f
is a
\operatorname{PL}
homeomorphism preserving the 0-section and commuting with the projection to
\Delta^{k}
.

(ii)

A morphism
\lambda\colon\Delta^{\ell}\to\Delta^{k}
is sent to the morphism
\lambda^{\#}
which assigns to
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}
the map such that the diagram below commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Delta^{k}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\#}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Define
\operatorname{PL}(n)
as the topological group
[
Mil57
, Theorem 3]
realising the simplicial group
\operatorname{PL}(n)_{\bullet}
.
Then define
\operatorname{BPL}(n)
using Proposition
6.2
.
Define
\operatorname{PL}
and
\operatorname{BPL}
as colimits, analogously to Definition
6.3
.

Equivalently, one can define
\operatorname{BPL}(n)
by first using the geometric bar construction level-wise on
\operatorname{PL}(n)_{\bullet}
to obtain a simplicial space
\operatorname{BPL}(n)_{\bullet}
, and then geometrically realising to obtain a space
\operatorname{BPL}(n)
; see e.g.
[
ERW19
, §1.2]
for the equivalence to the previous definition.

Remark 6.12
.

Consider the subgroup of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
consisting of
\operatorname{PL}
homeomorphisms (note this is
not
the definition of what we called
\operatorname{PL}(n)
above. In particular
\operatorname{PL}(n)
is a much larger set). One might think that this group, together with the subspace topology from
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
, is a realistic way to circumvent the construction above with simplicial groups. This would certainly produce a topological group that classifies
some
category of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
bundles, but we do not know whether it classifies the
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles defined above (it seems unlikely, cf. Remark
6.6
(
1
)).

The following proposition can now be viewed as an analogue of
Proposition
6.2
.

Proposition 6.13
.

For each
n\geq 0
, the space
\operatorname{BPL}(n)
is homotopy equivalent to a CW complex, and there exists a
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{PL}}
over
\operatorname{BPL}(n)
, such that for every
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\xi
over a CW complex, there is a
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle map
F\colon\xi\to\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{PL}}
, unique up to homotopy of
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle maps. In particular
F^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{PL}})\cong\xi
.

Proof.

In
[
Mil61
, p. 24]
, Milnor constructed a simplicial group
(\operatorname{PL}_{n})_{\bullet}
. It is defined similarly to
\operatorname{PL}(n)_{\bullet}
, but differs from what we have done in Definition
6.11
(i) by specifying that the maps
f
are rather
\operatorname{PL}
microbundle isomorphisms
of the trivial
\operatorname{PL}
microbundle over the simplex (in particular are
germs
of the types of maps
f
we are using). The simplicial space
(\operatorname{BPL}_{n})_{\bullet}
that results from the level-wise geometric bar construction is a classifying space for simplicial principal
(\operatorname{PL}_{n})_{\bullet}
-bundles. In
[
Mil61
, §5]
, Milnor showed that the geometric realisation
\operatorname{BPL}_{n}:=|(\operatorname{BPL}_{n})_{\bullet}|
is homotopy equivalent to a locally finite simplicial complex (so in particular a CW complex), and that there is a universal rank
n

\operatorname{PL}
microbundle over
\operatorname{BPL}_{n}
.

Kuiper-Lashof
[
KL66
, Theorem 1]
proved that each rank
n

\operatorname{PL}
microbundle is the underlying microbundle of a unique
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle. Apply this to the universal rank
n

\operatorname{PL}
microbundle over
\operatorname{BPL}_{n}
, to obtain a universal
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
\operatorname{BPL}_{n}
. The map
g\colon\operatorname{PL}(n)_{\bullet}\to(\operatorname{PL}_{n})_{\bullet}
, given by simplex-wise taking the germs of maps
f
as in Definition
6.11
(i), is a homotopy equivalence of simplicial sets
[
KL66
, Lemma 1.6(f)]
. This induces a homotopy equivalence
\operatorname{BPL}(n)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{BPL}_{n}
. Use this latter homotopy equivalence to pull back the universal rank
n

\operatorname{PL}
microbundle over
\operatorname{BPL}_{n}
to the desired base space
\operatorname{BPL}(n)
.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

(1)

The simplicial method used above for
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles can be used in the smooth and topological categories as well, giving a uniform treatment. The resulting classifying spaces for the smooth and topological categories are homotopy equivalent to the spaces
\operatorname{BO}(n)
and
\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
, defined earlier, by the universal property.

(2)

An alternative uniform proof of
Propositions
6.7
and
6.13
, for all three categories
\operatorname{TOP}
,
\operatorname{PL}
and
\operatorname{Diff}
simultaneously,
was given by Kirby-Siebenmann
[
KS77
, Essay IV, Proposition 8.1, p. 181]
. Instead of a simplicial approach, they use E. H. Brown’s theory of representability to obtain universal
\operatorname{CAT}

microbundles
(cf. Section
6.2
) over classifying spaces which are locally finite simplicial complexes. The theorems of Kister (Theorem
6.17
) and Kuiper-Lashof
[
KL66
, Theorem 1]
, together with the analogue for smooth microbundles, means that the Kirby-Siebenman universal
\operatorname{CAT}
microbundles can then be upgraded to universal
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles with the same base space, similarly to how we worked at the end of the proof of Proposition
6.13
.
The Kirby-Siebenmann proof also includes a statement relative to a closed subspace of the base.

6.2.
Microbundles

All smooth manifolds have tangent vector bundles and all smooth submanifolds have normal vector bundles. This is one reason that vector bundles, corresponding to the structure group
\operatorname{O}(n)
are the de facto bundle technology in the smooth category. A general difficulty we will face when talking about manifold transversality in Chapter
10
is that we will need to use some well-defined notion of normal structure for a submanifold and, outside of the smooth category, submanifolds do not necessarily admit normal vector bundles. However, various weaker bundle technologies have been developed, which replace this crucial concept in the topological category.

This subsection is devoted to a discussion of
microbundles
, which were introduced by Milnor in
[
Mil64
]
. The existence and uniqueness of tangent and (stable) normal microbundles leads to the existence and uniqueness of tangent and (stable) normal
\operatorname{TOP}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles, via Kister’s Theorem (Theorem
6.17
). Source material on microbundles is not hard to find in the literature, but has been included here for the convenience of the reader, in order for this survey to be more self-contained.

The interaction between the weaker structure groups
\operatorname{PL}(n)
and
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
for tangent and (stable) normal
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles, and the topological/
\operatorname{PL}
/smooth structures on the manifold itself are the topic of
smoothing theory
, to which we turn in Chapter
9
.

Definition 6.15
.

An
n
-dimensional
microbundle

\xi
consists of a base space
B
and a total space
E
sitting in a diagram

B\xrightarrow{i}E\xrightarrow{p}B,

such that
p\circ i=\operatorname{Id}_{B}
, and that is
locally trivial
in the following sense: for every point
b\in B
, there exists an open neighbourhood
U
of
B
, an open neighbourhood
V
of
i(b)
and a homeomorphism
\phi_{b}\colon V\to U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{b}}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{u\mapsto(u,0)}
\textstyle{U}
\textstyle{U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(u,v)\mapsto u}

commutes. We refer to
i
as the
inclusion map
of the microbundle and we refer to
p
as the
projection
.

Note that we only require neighbourhoods of the points
i(b)

to be trivial, and not all of the fibre
p^{-1}(b)
. In fact, we only care about
neighbourhoods
i(B)\subseteq E
, and declare two microbundles
B\xrightarrow{i}E\xrightarrow{p}B
and
B\xrightarrow{i^{\prime}}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p^{\prime}}B

to be
equivalent
, if
i(B)
and
i^{\prime}(B)
have homeomorphic neighbourhoods such that the homeomorphism commutes with both the inclusion map and the
restriction of the projection map.

Definition 6.16
.

Let
B\xrightarrow{i}E\xrightarrow{p}B
be a microbundle
\xi
and let
f\colon A\to B
be a map.
The
pullback
of
\xi
under
f
is the microbundle
f^{*}\xi
with total space

f^{*}E=\big\{(a,e)\in A\times E\mid f(a)=p(e)\big\},

projection
(f^{*}p)(a,e)=a
, and injection
(f^{*}i)(a)=\big(a,i(f(a))\big)
.
In the case that
f
is an inclusion, also consider the
microbundle
\xi|_{A}
, which has total space
p^{-1}(A)\subseteq E
, and projection
p|_{p^{-1}(A)}\colon p^{-1}(A)\to A
and injection
i_{A}\colon A\to p^{-1}(A)
are both the restrictions of
p
,
i
. In this case, the map of total spaces
(a,e)\mapsto e
gives a preferred isomorphism
f^{*}\xi
to
\xi|_{A}
.

A topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle clearly has an underlying microbundle.
Kister proved the surprising result that
every
microbundle over a manifold
is equivalent to such an underlying microbundle
[
Kis64
, Theorem 2 and Corollary 1]
.

Theorem 6.17
.

(Kister’s Theorem)

Let
B
be a manifold and
B\xrightarrow{i}E\xrightarrow{p}B
be an
n
-dimensional microbundle
\xi
. Then there exists an open set
F\subseteq E
containing
i(B)
such that
p|_{F}\colon F\to B
is the projection map of a topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle, whose
0
-section is
i
and whose underlying microbundle is
\xi
. Moreover, if
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are any two topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles over
B
such that the underlying microbundles are equivalent, then
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are isomorphic as topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles.

Every manifold admits a tangent microbundle.

Definition 6.18
.

The
tangent microbundle
of an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
is
the microbundle
M\xrightarrow{\Delta}M\times M\xrightarrow{(x,y)\mapsto x}M
where
\Delta
is the diagonal map. Kister’s theorem implies this corresponds to a unique
topological tangent bundle

\tau_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
, with corresponding
stable topological tangent bundle

\tau_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}
.

More subtle is the concept of a normal microbundle.

Definition 6.19
.

A
normal microbundle
of a submanifold
S
of a manifold
M
is
a microbundle
S\to E\to S
such that
E
is a neighbourhood of
S
in
M
and such that
S\to E
is the inclusion.

It is immediate from the definition of normal microbundle that the local flatness in the definition of a submanifold
S
is a necessary condition for the existence of a normal microbundle. For example
wild knots and the Alexander horned sphere do not admit normal microbundles. Indeed, it is generally far from straightforward to prove the existence of normal microbundles at all. Here is an existence and uniqueness result due to Stern
[
Ste75
, Theorem 4.5]
. See also
[
Hir66
]
,
[
Hir68
, p. 65]
, and
[
KS77
, Essay IV, Appendix A, p. 203]
.

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
M^{n+q}
be a manifold, and let
N^{n}\subseteq M^{n+q}
be a proper submanifold of codimension
q
. Suppose that
n\leq q+1+j
and
q\geq 5+j
for some
j=0,1,2
. Then
N
admits a normal microbundle restricting to a normal microbundle of
\partial N\subseteq\partial M
.

If in addition
n\leq q+j
, then this normal microbundle is unique up to isotopy.

Remark 6.21

(Unique up to isotopy)
.

For a submanifold
N\subseteq M
we say a normal microbundle
N\xrightarrow{i}\nu(N)\xrightarrow{p}N
is
unique up to isotopy
if whenever there is another normal microbundle
N\xrightarrow{i^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}(N)\xrightarrow{p^{\prime}}N
, there exists a microbundle equivalence
f
between
\nu(N)
and
\nu^{\prime}(N)
such that
p^{\prime}\circ f
is isotopic to
p
relative to
N
.

We exploit these theorems to define a stable topological normal structure on any closed manifold, that will play an important role in Chapter
9
.
Every closed smooth
n
-manifold
M
can be embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, for some
k
. The stable class of a normal vector bundle gives rise to
stable normal vector bundle of
M
, denoted
\mu_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BO}
. For
k
large enough, the embedding of
M
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is unique up to isotopy, and using this one can show that the stable normal bundle is uniquely determined up to isomorphism. We seek to adapt this construction to the topological category.

Consider that any closed
n
-manifold
M
can be embedded as a submanifold
M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
for large
m
(this follows e.g. from
[
Hat02
, Corollary A.9]
together with
[
Mil72
, Theorem 5]
). For large enough
m
, any two such embeddings are isotopic. For large enough
m
, Theorem
6.20
implies there is a normal microbundle
\xi
. After possibly increasing
m
further, the last sentence of Theorem
6.20
implies this normal microbundle
\xi
is unique. By Kister’s Theorem this defines a unique topological
\mathbb{R}^{m-n}
-bundle. We remove the dependence on
m
by passing to the stable bundle
\operatorname{TOP}(m-n)\subseteq\operatorname{TOP}
. Thus the process described gives a well-defined classifying map
\nu_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}
. Summarising, we have the following.

Definition 6.22
.

Given any closed
n
-manifold, the topological
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
-bundle
\nu_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}
, described above, is called the
stable topological normal bundle
. It is well-defined and unique.

The next example shows that outside the hypotheses of Theorem
6.20
, we should expect that normal microbundles can be very badly behaved.

Example 6.23
.

Normal microbundles do not necessarily exist.
Rourke and Sanderson
[
RS67
, Example 2]
construct
S^{19}
as a submanifold of a certain

28
-dimensional
\operatorname{PL}
manifold
M
in such a way that it does not admit a topological normal microbundle. The embedding is even piecewise linear.

Hirsch’s example of a 4-submanifold of
S^{7}
from
[
Hir68
, Theorem 4]
mentioned in Remark
5.4
also does not admit a normal microbundle. Such a normal microbundle would contain a
\operatorname{TOP}(3)
bundle, and every
\operatorname{TOP}(3)
bundle can be improved to an
\operatorname{O}(3)
bundle, which in turn contains a tubular neighbourhood. So it follows that there can be no normal microbundle in Hirsch’s example. Note that historically this deduction was not possible until Hatcher
[
Hat83
]
proved that
\operatorname{BO}(3)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(3)
is a homotopy equivalence.

Even when topological normal microbundles exist, they are not always unique: Rourke and Sanderson consider the smooth standard embedding
S^{18}\subseteq S^{27}

[
RS68
, Theorem 3.12]
and construct a certain normal microbundle
\xi
of
S^{18}\subseteq S^{27}
. The construction of
\xi
is such that if
\xi
were concordant to the trivial normal microbundle, this concordance would induce a normal microbundle structure back on the embedding
S^{19}\subseteq M^{28}
of the previous paragraph. As this is not possible,
\xi
is nontrivial. Note that the normal vector bundle
\nu S^{18}
of the standard embedding is trivial, so
S^{18}\subseteq S^{27}
admits at least two different normal microbundles.

The following theorem ensures the issues of the previous example are not seen in dimension 4.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
X
be a proper submanifold of a
4
-manifold
M
. Then
X
admits a normal microbundle. Moreover, if
\xi
is a normal microbundle of
X
, it is the underlying microbundle to a normal vector bundle.

Proof.

The existence of normal microbundles in ambient dimension 4 is an immediate consequence of the existence of normal vector bundles (Theorem
5.14
), and this is the only proof of which we are aware for this fact. (It would be interesting to know of a more elementary proof.)

We denote by
n
the codimension of
X
in
M
.
Given a normal microbundle
\xi
, we apply Kister’s theorem
6.17
to obtain an embedded
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle with underlying microbundle
\xi
. For
n\leq 3
, the homotopy fibre
\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n)
for the forgetful map
\operatorname{BO}(n)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
is contractible; see Proposition
7.5
for the relevant citations.
Using these facts, and checking the obstructions in each of the cases
n=0,1,2,3,4
, we see in each case the embedded topological
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle can be upgraded to an embedded vector bundle. Choose such a vector bundle refinement. By restricting to an open disc bundle and rescaling we can ensure this internal linear bundle is extendable and thus is a normal vector bundle in the sense of Definition
5.12
.
∎

We will make use of our discussion of normal microbundles in Chapter
10
on topological transversality.

Chapter 7
Stiefel-Whitney classes

The well-known treatment of Stiefel-Whitney classes in Milnor-Stasheff
[
MS74
]
is for vector bundles, that is bundles over a space
B
with linear transition functions. In this chapter we discuss the analogous characteristic classes for
\operatorname{TOP}
and
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles. These arise, in particular, for tangent bundles of
\operatorname{PL}
and topological manifolds respectively.
Throughout this chapter we will use the terminology
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
, where

\operatorname{CAT}\in\{\operatorname{Diff},\operatorname{PL},\operatorname{TOP}\}.

When
\operatorname{CAT}=\operatorname{TOP}
, this will mean an
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle with structure group
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
. When
\operatorname{CAT}=\operatorname{PL}
, this will mean
\operatorname{PL}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles in the sense of Definition
6.9
. When
\operatorname{CAT}=\operatorname{Diff}
, we will mean an
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle with structure group
\operatorname{O}(n)
, taking advantage of the structure group reduction described in Remark
6.6
.

Generalising work of Thom
[
Tho52
]
in the case of orthogonal structure group,
Fadell
[
Fad65
, Definition 6.1]
(see also Stern
[
Ste75
]
) defined Stiefel-Whitney classes of
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundles, where
\operatorname{CAT}\in\{\operatorname{Diff},\operatorname{PL},\operatorname{TOP}\}
.
As we shall see, the definitions of Stiefel-Whitney classes in the
\operatorname{PL}
and
\operatorname{TOP}
cases are analogous to the classical definition for vector bundles.

Remark 7.1
.

Even more generally, one can also develop Stiefel-Whitney classes for spherical fibrations, using an analogous definition. Passing to the underlying
S^{n-1}
-bundle of a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle, the spherical fibration definition will recover all definitions we give below. However, we have chosen not work in this level of generality.

Let
\xi=(p\colon E\to B)
be a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over some topological space
B
. Let
E_{0}
denote the complement in the total space
E
of the zero section. Recall that the Thom isomorphism
[
Tho52
]
is an isomorphism

\Phi\colon H^{i}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{n+i}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2).

Fadell
[
Fad65
, Theorem 5.2]
checked that the Thom isomorphism holds in the present context, with
\operatorname{PL}(n)
or
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
structure group.
The Steenrod squares are homomorphisms

\operatorname{Sq}^{i}\colon H^{n}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{n+i}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2).

Here, let us recall that given a pair of spaces
(X,A)
, for each
i\geq 0
and for each
n\geq 0
the Steenrod square
\operatorname{Sq}^{i}
is a homomorphism
\operatorname{Sq}^{i}\colon H^{n}(X,A;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{n+i}(X,A;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. The Steenrod squares have the following properties, which we will use; see e.g.
[
Ste62
]
.

(1)

The
\operatorname{Sq}^{i}
are natural with respect to maps of pairs
f\colon(X,A)\to(Y,B)
, for all
i\geq 0
.

(2)

\operatorname{Sq}^{0}=\operatorname{Id}
;

(3)

\operatorname{Sq}^{i}(x)=0
for
x\in H^{n}(X,A;\mathbb{Z}/2)
with
n<i
;

(4)

\operatorname{Sq}^{i}(x)=x\cup x
for
x\in H^{i}(X,A;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Let
1\in H^{0}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)
denote the unit of the cohomology ring
H^{*}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Definition 7.2

(Stiefel-Whitney classes
[
Fad65
, Definition 6.1]
,
[
Ste75
, p. 262]
)
.

The
i
th Stiefel-Whitney class of
\xi
is

w_{i}(\xi):=\Phi^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Sq}^{i}\circ\Phi(1)\in H^{i}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2).

The definition uses the sequence of maps

H^{0}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\Phi,\cong}H^{n}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Sq}^{i}}H^{n+i}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\Phi^{-1},\cong}H^{i}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2).

Since
\operatorname{Sq}^{j}\colon H^{n}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{n+j}(E,E_{0};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the zero map for
j>n
, by the third property of Steenrod squares, it follows that
w_{j}(\xi)=0
for
j>n
.

We now restrict ourselves to bundles over spaces that are homotopy equivalent to CW complexes. In this context it was shown by
[
Fad65
]
and Stern
[
Ste75
, Theorem 2.0]
that the Stiefel-Whitney classes
w_{i}(\xi)
for
0\leq i\leq n
satisfy the following properties:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
B
be a space homotopy equivalent to a CW complex and let
\xi=(p\colon E\to B)
be a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
B
.
Define the total Stiefel-Whitney class

w(\xi):=\sum_{i=0}^{n}w_{i}(\xi)\in H^{*}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2).

(1)

For a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle map
f=(f_{E},f_{B})\colon\xi\to\eta
, which consists of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\eta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\eta),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(\xi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{B}}

we have that
f_{B}^{*}(w(\eta))=w(\xi)
.

(2)

If
\xi=\eta^{q}\oplus\varepsilon^{n-q}
, where
\eta
is a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{q}
-bundle over
B
and
\varepsilon^{n-q}
is a trivial
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n-q}
-bundle over
B
, then
w(\xi)=w(\eta)
.

(3)

For each
n
there is a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle such that
w_{n}(\xi)\neq 0
.

Under the assumption that either
\operatorname{CAT}\in\{\operatorname{PL},\operatorname{Diff}\}
or
\operatorname{CAT}=\operatorname{TOP}
and
q\neq 4,5
, Stern also proved that the properties in Proposition
7.3
characterise the Stiefel-Whitney classes. We are not sure whether the assumption that
q\neq 4,5
can now be removed in the
\operatorname{TOP}
case using Quinn’s work.

Recall the universal
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}}
over
\operatorname{BCAT}(n)
from Propositions
6.7
and
6.13
. Here we are abusing notation, and using the deformation retract
\operatorname{Diff}(n)\simeq\operatorname{O}(n)
(Remark
6.6
) to conflate these structure groups. We denote the
universal Stiefel-Whitney classes
by

\overline{w}_{k}^{\operatorname{CAT}}:=w_{k}(\gamma_{n}^{\operatorname{CAT}})\in H^{k}(\operatorname{BCAT}(n);\mathbb{Z}/2),

for some
n\geq k
. We also write
\overline{w}_{k}^{\operatorname{CAT}}\colon\operatorname{BCAT}(n)\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,k)
for the corresponding map to the Eilenberg-Maclane space. For
F\colon B\to\operatorname{BCAT}(n)
, classifying a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle
\xi
, we have by Proposition
7.3
that

w_{k}(\xi)=F^{*}(\overline{w}_{k}^{\operatorname{CAT}})\in H^{k}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2).

The definitions for each value of
\operatorname{CAT}
are compatible in the following sense.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\xi
be a
\operatorname{CAT}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
B
, for some
\operatorname{CAT}\in\{\operatorname{Diff},\operatorname{PL}\}
. Let
\xi_{\operatorname{TOP}}
be the underlying
\operatorname{TOP}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle. Then
w_{k}(\xi)=w_{k}(\xi_{\operatorname{TOP}})
for every
k\geq 0
.
In fact, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BCAT}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BTOP}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathbb{Z}/2,k).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\operatorname{TOP}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w}_{k}^{\operatorname{CAT}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w}_{k}^{\operatorname{TOP}}}

commutes up to homotopy.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the fact that the definitions in all three cases are directly analogous; cf.
[
Tho52
, Théorème III.8]
and
[
Fad65
, Theorem 6.10]
, which consider the case of tangent bundles.
∎

From now on we will often take the previous proposition to heart, and omit the
\operatorname{CAT}
superscript, writing
\overline{w}_{k}
instead of
\overline{w}_{k}^{\operatorname{CAT}}
. For the rest of this chapter this will in any case only be used with
\operatorname{CAT}=\operatorname{TOP}
.

Recall that
\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n)
is by definition the homotopy fibre of
\operatorname{BO}(n)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
.

Proposition 7.5
.

\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n)
is contractible for
n\leq 3
, while for
n\geq 4
there is a 5-connected map
\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n)\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3)
.

Proof.

According to
[
KS77
, Essay V, Section 5.0, p. 246]
, the homotopy fibre
\operatorname{TOP}(2)/\operatorname{O}(2)
is contractible,
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{TOP}(3)/\operatorname{O}(3))=0
for
i\geq 4
, and
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{TOP}(3)/\operatorname{O}(3))\cong\pi_{i}(\operatorname{Diff}(D^{3},\partial D^{3}))
for
i\geq 4
. The latter group is trivial for all
i
, by Hatcher’s theorem
[
Hat83
]
. Thus all the homotopy groups of
\operatorname{TOP}(3)/\operatorname{O}(3)
vanish, and therefore
\operatorname{BO}(3)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(3)
induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BO}(3))\to\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BTOP}(3))
for all
i
.
Since
\operatorname{BO}(3)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(3)
is a map between spaces homotopy equivalent to a CW complex, we deduce that this map is a homotopy equivalence by Whitehead’s theorem. Thus
\operatorname{TOP}(3)/\operatorname{O}(3)
is the homotopy fibre of a homotopy equivalence and so is contractible.

The reference
[
KS77
, Essay V, Section 5.0, p. 246]
also includes the statement that for
n\geq 5
and
i\leq 7
we have
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{O},\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n))=0
. In addition
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{O})\cong\pi_{i}(K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3))
for
i\leq 6
, with the isomorphism induced by the map
\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{O}\to\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{PL}\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3)
, which is therefore a 5-connected map. The homotopy equivalence
\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{PL}\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3)
is from
[
KS77
, Essay IV, Section 10.12, p. 200]
. Since the composition of two 5-connected maps is 5-connected, it follows that there is a
5
-connected map
\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n)\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3)
for
n\geq 5
, as claimed.

It remains to consider
n=4
. For this we appeal to
[
FQ90
, Theorem 8.7A]
, which states that
\operatorname{TOP}(4)/\operatorname{O}(4)\to\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{O}
is 5-connected. Combined with the fact already discussed that there is a 5-connected map
\operatorname{TOP}/\operatorname{O}\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3)
, we obtain the sought-for 5-connected map
\operatorname{TOP}(4)/\operatorname{O}(4)\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,3)
.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

(1)

For
n\geq 2
,
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{BTOP}(n))=\mathbb{Z}/2
.

(2)

The corresponding unique homotopically nontrivial map
\operatorname{BTOP}(n)\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)
is the universal first Stiefel-Whitney class
\overline{w}_{1}
.

(3)

We have that
\pi_{2}(\operatorname{BTOP}(2))\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

(4)

For
n\geq 3
,
\pi_{2}(\operatorname{BTOP}(n))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
.

(5)

The corresponding unique homotopically nontrivial map
\operatorname{BTOP}(n)\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)
is the universal second Stiefel-Whitney class
\overline{w}_{2}
.

Proof.

For
n\leq 3
we have
\operatorname{TOP}(n)\simeq\operatorname{O}(n)
by Proposition
7.5
, and so the homotopy groups of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
are isomorphic to those of
\operatorname{O}(n)
. For
n\geq 4
we have the long exact sequence in homotopy groups:

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n))

\displaystyle\to\pi_{2}(\operatorname{BO}(n))\to\pi_{2}(\operatorname{BTOP}(n))\to

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n))

\displaystyle\to\pi_{1}(\operatorname{BO}(n))\to\pi_{1}(\operatorname{BTOP}(n))\to\{\ast\}.

Since
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n))=0
for
i=1,2
by Proposition
7.5
, we deduce that
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BO}(n))\cong\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BTOP}(n))
for
i=1,2
, with the map induced by the canonical forgetful map. Since
\pi_{i}(\operatorname{BO}(n))\cong\pi_{i-1}(\operatorname{O}(n))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
for
i=1,2
, the result follows from this and Proposition
7.4
.
∎

We see that
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
has two connected components, which are homotopy equivalent because
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
is a topological group, and
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{TOP}(n),\operatorname{Id})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
for
n\geq 3
.

Definition 7.7
.

We define
\operatorname{STOP}(n)
to be the subgroup of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
consisting of orientation preserving homeomorphisms.
We define
\operatorname{TOPSpin}(n)
to be the universal cover of
\operatorname{STOP}(n)
. Define
\operatorname{STOP}
and
\operatorname{TOPSpin}
as corresponding colimits.

This definition is analogous to the definition of
\operatorname{SO}(n)
as the subgroup of
\operatorname{O}(n)
of orientation preserving orthogonal matrices, and of
\operatorname{Spin}(n)
as the connected double cover of
\operatorname{SO}(n)
; this is the universal cover for
n\geq 3
.

Theorem 7.8
.

The topological group
\operatorname{STOP}(n)
is the connected component of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
containing the identity.

Proof.

We saw above that
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
has two connected components. For a homeomorphism
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and another such homeomorphism
g
, if
f
and
g
are isotopic, then
f
and
g
are either both orientation preserving (o.p.) or both orientation reversing (o.r.). The map
\pi_{0}(\operatorname{TOP}(n))\to\{\text{o.p.},\text{o.r.}\}
is a surjective map from a set with two elements to another set with two elements, hence is a bijection.

(Alternatively, the theorem can be seen as a consequence of the Stable Homeomorphism Theorem
4.3
, which says that every orientation-preserving homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is stable. Using that every homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is the identity on some subset is isotopic to the identity, via the (inverted) Alexander trick, as in Corollary
4.5
, we deduce the result. The computations of the homotopy type of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)/\operatorname{O}(n)
also used the Stable Homeomorphism Theorem, so this alternative proof is not independent of the first.)
∎

We have the following commutative diagram of classifying spaces, together with the universal Stiefel-Whitney classes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BSpin}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BSO}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BO}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BTOP}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w}_{1}}

The horizontal maps in the bottom row induce isomorphisms on
\pi_{1}
, while the horizontal maps in the middle row induce isomorphisms on
\pi_{2}
.
Thus up to homotopy equivalence,
\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)
is the 1-connected cover of
\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
, and
\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)
is the 2-connected cover.
In other words,
\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)
is the homotopy fibre of
\overline{w}_{1}
and
\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)
is the homotopy fibre of
\overline{w}_{2}
.

The analogous statements hold in the case of
\operatorname{O}(n)
.

Definition 7.9
.

Let
M
be a space homotopy equivalent to a CW complex and let
\xi
be a
\operatorname{TOP}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
M
classified by a map which we also denote
\xi\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
. An
orientation
on
\xi
is a lift
M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)
of
\xi
, and two orientations are equivalent if the lifts are homotopic over
\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
. If an orientation exists then we say
\xi
is
orientable
.

Proposition 7.10
.

An orientation for an
n
-manifold
M
is equivalent to an orientation on the topological tangent bundle
\tau_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
. In particular
M
is orientable if and only if
\tau_{M}
is orientable.

Proof.

An orientation for
M
is equivalent to a homology orientation for
M
, i.e. a coherent choice of generators of
H_{n}(M,M\setminus\{x\};\mathbb{Z})
, for
x\in M
. In turn, a homology orientation for
M
is equivalent to a coherent system of orientations of the fibre of the tangent microbundle
M\xrightarrow{\Delta}M\times M\xrightarrow{\pr_{1}}M
, rel. the zero section
\Delta(M)
. This is because both are, by definition, a coherent system of generators of
H_{n}(M,M\setminus\{x\};\mathbb{Z})
, for each
x\in M
. See
[
MS74
, Lemma 11.6]
for details.

Next, the latter notion is equivalent, via Kister’s theorem
[
Kis64
]
and excision, to a coherent choice of generators of
H_{n}(F_{x},F_{x}\setminus\{0\};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Here,
F_{x}
is the fibre over
x
in the topological tangent
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle of
M
; see
[
MS74
, Lemma 11.7]
, but replace the exponential map with the embedding from Kister’s theorem.

To see that a coherent choice of isomorphisms
H_{n}(F_{x},F_{x}\setminus\{0\};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is equivalent to a choice of lift
M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)
, recall that the two connected components of
\operatorname{TOP}(n)
correspond to whether a homeomorphism preserves or changes a fixed generator of
H_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\};\mathbb{Z})
, so the structure group reduces to
\operatorname{STOP}(n)
if and only if there is a coherent choice of generators for the homology groups
H_{n}(F_{x},F_{x}\setminus\{0\};\mathbb{Z})
, for
x\in M
.
∎

Definition 7.11
.

For an
n
-manifold
M
, with topological tangent bundle
\tau_{M}
, we define
w_{i}(M):=w_{i}(\tau_{M})
.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
M
be a space homotopy equivalent to a CW complex and let
\xi
be a
\operatorname{TOP}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
M
. The bundle
\xi
is orientable if and only if
w_{1}(\xi)=0
.
In particular, by Proposition
7.10
, a manifold
M
is orientable if and only if
w_{1}(M)=0
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)\xrightarrow{\overline{w}_{1}}K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)
is a fibration sequence, we have an exact sequence of pointed sets

[M,\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)]\to[M,\operatorname{BTOP}(n)]\to[M,K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)]\cong H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2).

The bundle
\xi
is orientable if and only if the classifying map
\xi\in[M,\operatorname{BTOP}(n)]
is homotopic to a map in the image of
[M,\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)]
. The latter is equivalent to
\overline{w}_{1}\circ\xi\in[M,K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)]
being null-homotopic, using the sequence.
Translating to cohomology groups this is equivalent to
w_{1}(\xi)=\xi^{*}(\overline{w}_{1})=0
. Here we used that
w_{1}(\xi)
is equal to the pullback of the universal bundle along the classifying map for
\xi
.
∎

Definition 7.13
.

Let
M
be a space homotopy equivalent to a CW complex and let
\xi
be a
\operatorname{TOP}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
M
classified by a map which we also denote
\xi\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
. Suppose that
w_{1}(M)=0
, so
\xi
is orientable. A
spin structure
on
\xi
is a lift
M\to\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)
of
\xi
, and two spin structures are equivalent if the lifts are homotopy equivalent. If a spin structure exists then we say
\xi
is
spin
.

Now suppose that
M
is a topological
n
-manifold with topological tangent bundle classified by
\tau_{M}\colon M\to\operatorname{BTOP}(n)
. Suppose that
w_{1}(M)=0
, so
M
is orientable. A
spin structure
on
M
is a spin structure on
\tau_{M}
. If a spin structure on
\tau_{M}
exists then we say
M
is
spin
.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
M
be a space homotopy equivalent to a CW complex and let
\xi
be a
\operatorname{TOP}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
-bundle over
M
. Suppose that
\xi
is orientable.
Then
\xi
is spin if and only if
w_{2}(\xi)=0
.
In particular, an orientable manifold
M
is spin if and only if
w_{2}(M)=0
.

Proof.

This holds because
\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)\to\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)\xrightarrow{\overline{w}_{2}}K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)
is a fibration sequence. Thus a lift of
\xi\colon M\to\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)
to
\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)
exists if and only if
\overline{w}_{2}\circ\tau_{M}
is null-homotopic, i.e. if and only if
w_{2}(\xi)=0
.
Here we are again using that a fibration sequence gives rise to an exact sequence of sets

[M,\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)]\to[M,\operatorname{BSTOP}(n)]\xrightarrow{}[M,K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)]\cong H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2),

and hence a lift to
\operatorname{BTOPSpin}(n)
exists if and only if
w_{2}(\xi)=\xi^{*}(\overline{w}_{2})=0
.
∎

We now recall the Wu classes and the Wu formulae for the Stiefel-Whitney classes of compact manifolds. The key point here is that while the treatment in
[
MS74
, Chapter 11]
is presented for smooth manifolds, in fact it uses only Poincaré duality and algebraic topology, so works just as well for topological manifolds. This has been observed by Thom
[
Tho52
]
and Fadell
[
Fad65
]
.
We proceed to summarise the treatment in Milnor-Stasheff.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-manifold with
\mathbb{Z}/2
-fundamental class
[M,\partial M]\in H_{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
Consider the homomorphism

\displaystyle\theta\colon H^{n-k}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}/2

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\operatorname{Sq}^{k}(x),[M,\partial M]\rangle.

By Poincaré duality, there is a unique class
v_{k}(M)\in H^{k}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
with

\langle v_{k}(M)\cup x,[M,\partial M]\rangle=\theta(x)

for all
x\in H^{n-k}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. In fact
v_{k}(M)\cup x=\operatorname{Sq}^{k}(x)\in H^{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
for every
x\in H^{n-k}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Definition 7.15
.

The class
v_{k}(M)
is the
k
th Wu class of
M
.

Proposition 7.16
.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-manifold. Then the Wu formulae

w_{k}(M)=\sum_{i+j=k}\operatorname{Sq}^{i}(v_{j})

hold for
k=0,\dots,n
.

Proof.

The proof in
[
MS74
, Theorem 11.11, Lemma 11.13, and Theorem 11.14]
, which relies on
[
MS74
, Theorem 11.11 and Lemma 11.13
]
, uses only Poincaré duality and products from algebraic topology, and so proceeds exactly as in Milnor-Stasheff.
∎

Here is a sample, and often used, application for the second Stiefel-Whitney class.

Definition 7.17
.

Let
M
be a compact, oriented
n
-manifold and
\Sigma^{n-2}\subseteq M
a proper submanifold. We say
\Sigma
is
characteristic
if
\operatorname{PD}(w_{2}(M))=j_{*}([\Sigma])\in H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, where
j_{*}\colon H_{n-2}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the inclusion-induced map.

Proposition 7.18
.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-manifold and
\Sigma\subseteq M
a proper submanifold. Then
w_{2}(M\setminus\Sigma)=0
if and only if
\Sigma
is chracteristic.

Proof.

Throughout the proof,
\mathbb{Z}/2
-coefficients are understood. By the Collar Neighbourhood Theorem (
2.16
), we may take a boundary collar on
M
that restricts on
\Sigma
to a boundary collar on
\Sigma
.
Set
K:=M\setminus(\partial M\times[0,1))
, the complement of the open collar on
\partial M
; note
K
is compact. Write
\nu\Sigma
for a open tubular neighbourhood of
\Sigma
. Tubular neighbourhoods were proved to exist in codimension 2 when
n\neq 4
by Kirby-Siebenmann
[
KS75
]
and for
n=4
by Freedman-Quinn (Theorem
5.16
). Next we draw a diagram, then define the maps and justify that it has exact rows, is commutative, and that the variously claimed isomorphisms indeed are so.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M,M\setminus\nu\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M\setminus\nu\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(K,K\setminus\nu\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(K\setminus\nu\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-2}(\partial M\cup\nu\Sigma,\partial M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-2}(M,\partial M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-2}(\nu\Sigma,\partial M\cap\nu\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-2}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\mathcal{O}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\mathcal{O}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
incl
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
incl
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}}
incl
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The top row is a section of the long exact sequence of the pair
(M,M\setminus\nu\Sigma)
, and the central row is a section of the long exact sequence of the pair
(K,K\setminus\nu\Sigma)
, thus both are exact. The downwards maps from the top row to the middle row are by definition the maps induced by inclusion, which is a homotopy equivalence, justifying these isomorphisms and the fact that the subdiagram consisting of the top two rows commutes.

The class
\mathcal{O}
is the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-orientation class; see
[
Bre97
, §VI.8]
.
Replacing
M
by
M\setminus\partial M
, we obtain a (non-compact) 4-manifold
M^{\prime}
with empty boundary.
By
[
Bre97
, §VI Theorem 8.3]
, capping with
\mathcal{O}
induces isomorphisms

H^{2}(K,L)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(M^{\prime}\setminus L,M^{\prime}\setminus K),

both for
L:=K\setminus\nu\Sigma
and for
L:=\varnothing
. Thus, we obtain isomorphisms

H^{2}(K,K\setminus\nu\Sigma))\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(\partial M\times(0,1)\cup\nu\Sigma,\partial M\times(0,1))\cong H_{n-2}(\partial M\cup\nu\Sigma,\partial M)

and

H^{2}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(M^{\prime},\partial M\times(0,1))\cong H_{n-2}(M,\partial M).

These define the middle vertical maps labelled
-\cap\mathcal{O}
. By naturality of the cap product, the left middle square commutes.
The commutativity of the bottom left square is clear, as is the isomorphism of the left-bottom arrow.
We also note that the composite of the top two central downwards arrows is the Poincaré-Lefschetz duality isomorphism
PD\colon H^{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-2}(M,\partial M)
.
Having established the relevant properties of the diagram, we now prove the lemma.

First note that by naturality of Stiefel-Whitney classes, we have
\alpha(w_{2}(M))=w_{2}(M\setminus\nu\Sigma)
. Note as well that
H_{n-2}(\partial M\cup\overline{\nu}\Sigma,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
and generated by the image of
[\Sigma]
.

For one direction of the lemma, assume that
w_{2}(M\setminus\Sigma)=0
. By exactness of the top row, this implies
w_{2}(M)
lies in the image of
\beta
. But as
H^{2}(M,M\setminus\nu\Sigma))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and
w_{2}(M)\neq 0
, this implies the generator of
\mathbb{Z}/2
is sent to
w_{2}(M)
by
\beta
. By commutativity of the diagram, we get
\operatorname{PD}(w_{2}(M))\equiv j_{*}([\Sigma])\in H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Conversely, assume that
\operatorname{PD}(w_{2}(M))\equiv j_{*}([\Sigma])\in H_{n-2}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By commutativity of the diagram, this implies
w_{2}(M)
is in the image of
\beta
.
Thus
w_{2}(M\setminus\nu\Sigma)=\alpha(w_{2}(M))=0
by exactness of the top row.
∎

Remark 7.19
.

We note that the previous proposition is valid when none of the manifolds involved is orientable and also
\Sigma
is closed.
We also note that the proof given above is fairly robust and could be easily adapted to other Stiefel-Whitney classes, provided the tubular neighbourhood conditions are met.
For example, the same idea shows that the complement of a codimension 1 submanifold is orientable if and only if that submanifold is Poincaré dual to the first Stiefel-Whitney class.

Chapter 8
Intersection forms and smooth 4-manifolds

In this chapter we introduce and study one of the most interesting invariants of 4-manifolds, namely the intersection form.
Later, in Theorem
12.2
we will see that any unimodular symmetric form over
\mathbb{Z}
occurs as the intersection form of a closed oriented 4-manifold. In contrast we will see in this chapter that not all unimodular
symmetric form over
\mathbb{Z}
can be realised as the intersection forms of closed oriented
smooth
4-manifolds.

8.1.
Intersection forms

We start out with the definition of the intersection form.

Definition 8.1
.

(1)

Given a finitely generated abelian group
H
we write
\operatorname{F}\!H:=H/\mbox{torsion subgroup}
.

(2)

Given a compact oriented
n
-manifold
M
we denote by
[M]\in H_{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
its fundamental class. Given a decomposition
\partial M=A\cup B
where
A
and
B
are compact codimension-zero submanifolds of
\partial M
with
A\cap B=\partial A=\partial B
we denote the Poincaré duality isomorphism by
