(2)

Suppose we have a chain of subspaces
Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X\subseteq M
such that the closure of
Z
is contained in the interior of
Y
. Then the inclusion
(X\setminus Z,Y\setminus Z)\to(X,Y)
induces an isomorphism in homology and cohomology i.e. for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

H_{k}(X\setminus Z,Y\setminus Z;A)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{k}(X,Y;A)\quad\mbox{and}\quad H^{k}(X\setminus Z,Y\setminus Z;A)\xleftarrow{\cong}H^{k}(X,Y;A)

(3)

If
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}\subseteq M
and
V_{1}\subseteq V_{2}\subseteq M
are open subsets in
M
, then there are long exact sequences in homology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k}(U_{1}\cap V_{1},U_{2}\cap V_{2};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}H_{k}(U_{1},U_{2};A)\\
\oplus\\
H_{k}(V_{1},V_{2};A)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k}(U_{1}\cup V_{2},U_{2}\cup V_{2};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k-1}(U_{1}\cap V_{1},U_{2}\cap V_{2};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}

and cohomology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k-1}(U_{1}\cap V_{1},U_{2}\cap V_{2};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}(U_{1}\cup V_{1},U_{2}\cup V_{2};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}H^{k}(U_{1},U_{2};A)\\
\oplus\\
H^{k}(V_{1},V_{2};A)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}(U_{1}\cap V_{2},U_{2}\cap V_{2};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

(4)

Suppose the inclusion
Y\to X
is a homotopy equivalence, then the inclusion induced maps

H_{k}(Y;A)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{k}(X;A)\quad\mbox{and}\quad H^{k}(Y;A)\xleftarrow{\cong}H^{k}(X;A)

are isomorphisms.

(5)

Let
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}\subseteq\ldots
be a sequence of open sets in
M
and let
U=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}U_{i}
, then for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the inclusions induce an isomorphism

{\varinjlim}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}H_{k}(U_{i};A)\,\,\xrightarrow{\cong}\,\,H_{k}(U;A).

The proofs are essentially the same as in the classical case. Therefore we will only sketch the arguments and focus on what is different. We also warn the reader that we give the “philosophically wrong proof” of statement (4). This is due to the fact that we developed the theory of twisted coefficients only for inclusions and hence a homotopy inverse does not fit in our theory. Therefore statement (4) will be deduced in a slightly round-about way using the following elementary lemma
[
Bre97
, Theorem III.3.4 & remark after proof]
.

Lemma A.18
.

(Covering Homotopy Theorem)

Given a covering
p\colon\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
, a homotopy
H\colon Y\times I\rightarrow X
, and a lift
\widetilde{h}\colon Y\to\widetilde{X}
of
H(-,0)
, there exists a unique lift
\widetilde{H}\colon Y\times I\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
of
H
with
\widetilde{h}=\widetilde{H}(-,0)
.

Proof of Theorem
A.17
.

Recall that
p\colon\widetilde{M}\rightarrow M
denotes the universal cover.
For statement (1) we consider the short exact sequence
0\to C_{*}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to C_{*}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to 0
of
free

\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules.
Since the modules are free the sequence stays exact after applying the functors
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}-
and
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(-,A)
.

Recall the proof of statement (2) and (3) in the classical case as in
[
Bre97
, Chapter IV.17]
. The main ingredient is to show that the inclusion of chain complexes
C^{\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to C^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
induces an isomorphism on homology
[
Bre97
, Theorem IV.17.7]
. Here
\mathcal{U}
is an open cover of
X
and
C^{\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is the free abelian group generated by simplices
\sigma
for which there is a
U\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\sigma\colon\Delta^{*}\to p^{-1}(U)
.
This is done by defining the barycentric subdivision
\Upsilon_{*}\colon C_{*}(\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow C_{*}(\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z})
and a chain homotopy
T
between
\Upsilon_{*}
and the identity
[
Bre97
, Lemma IV.17.1]
. The important thing for us to observe is that both maps are natural
[
Bre97
, Claim (1) in proof of Lemma IV.17.1]
. Hence for a twisted chain
\Upsilon(e\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\sigma):=e\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Upsilon(\sigma)
is well-defined, because

\displaystyle\Upsilon(e\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\gamma\sigma)

\displaystyle=e\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Upsilon(\gamma\sigma)

\displaystyle=e\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\gamma\Upsilon(\sigma)\quad\mbox{(naturality of $\Upsilon$)}

\displaystyle=e\gamma\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Upsilon(\sigma)=\Upsilon(e\gamma\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\sigma).

The same holds for
T
and from now on one can follow the classical proofs. Alternatively, one could invoke Lemma
A.4
.

Next we prove statement (4).
Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y\subseteq X
be a homotopy inverse of the inclusion and
H\colon X\times I\rightarrow X
a homotopy between
\operatorname{Id}_{X}
and
f
. Since
p\colon\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
is a covering and
\operatorname{Id}_{{\widetilde{X}}}
is a lift of
H(p(-),0)
, we get by Lemma
A.3
a lift
\widetilde{H}\colon\widetilde{X}\times I\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
of the homotopy
H
. One easily verifies that the inclusion
\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{X}
induces a homotopy equivalence where a homotopy inverse is given by
\widetilde{H}(-,1)
. Hence the inclusion induced map
H_{k}(C_{*}(\widetilde{Y};\mathbb{Z}))\to H_{k}(C_{*}(\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z}))
is an isomorphism for every
k
. Thus the claim follows from Lemma
A.4
.

The proof of Statement (5) is almost verbatim the same proof as in the classical case.
∎

A.4.
The main technical theorem

Given a group
\pi
we can view
\mathbb{Z}
as a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module with trivial
\pi
-action. We denote this module by
\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}}
. Let
p\colon\widetilde{M}\to M
be the covering projection. We have the following useful lemma, concerning the chain map
C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})\to C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
defined by
k\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\widetilde{\sigma}\mapsto k\cdot p(\sigma)
.

Lemma A.19
.

Given any subset
X\subseteq M
the chain map above is an isomorphism between
C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
and
C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, and induces one between
C^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and
C^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
, where
C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and
C^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
are the untwisted singular chain complexes.

Proof.

The isomorphism is given by lifting a simplex, which is always possible since a simplex is simply connected. If one has two different choices of lifts, then they differ by an element in
\pi
. But the action of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
on
\mathbb{Z}
is trivial and hence this indeterminacy vanishes.
∎

We will keep the notational difference between
C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and
C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
to emphasise where our simplices live.

As above let
R
be a ring and let
A
be an
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
-bimodule.
Let
K\subseteq M
be a compact subset of
M
. We define the (twisted) Cech cohomology groups

\displaystyle\check{H}^{p}(K;A):=\lim_{\underset{K\subseteq U\subseteq M}{\longrightarrow}}H^{p}(U;A),

where the direct limit runs over all open sets in
M
containing
K
. Since cohomology is contravariant, we define the order on open sets in the reversed way i.e.
U\leq V
if
V\subseteq U
.

Now we assume that
M
is oriented. Being oriented gives us, for any compact subset
K\subseteq M
, a preferred element
\theta_{K}\in H_{n}(M,M\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})\cong H_{n}(M,M\setminus K;\mathbb{Z})
, which restricts for all
x\in K
to the generator in
H_{n}(M,M\setminus\left\{x\right\};\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
.

For any open set
U\subseteq M
containing
K
,
the inclusion induced map
H_{n}(U,U\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})\to H_{n}(M,M\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})

is an isomorphism by Theorem
A.17
(2).
Let
\ex_{U}\colon H_{n}(M,M\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})\to H_{n}(U,U\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
be the inverse of this inclusion induced isomorphism i.e. if
j\colon U\rightarrow M
is the inclusion then
j_{*}\circ\ex_{U}=\operatorname{Id}
.
We then obtain a map

\displaystyle D_{U}\colon H^{p}(U;A)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H_{n}(M,M\setminus K;A)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto j_{*}(\phi\acap\ex_{U}(\theta_{K})).

Given another open set
V\subseteq U
denote by
i\colon V\rightarrow U
the inclusion. Then one easily calculates:

\displaystyle\operatorname{PD}_{V}(i^{*}\phi)=j_{*}i_{*}(i^{*}\phi\acap\ex_{V}(\theta_{K}))=j_{*}(\phi\acap i_{*}\ex_{V}(\theta_{K}))=j_{*}(\phi\acap\ex_{U}(\theta_{K}))=\operatorname{PD}_{U}(\phi).

Or, in other words, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p}(U;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K;A).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p}(V;A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}_{V}}

By the universal property of the direct limit we obtain the
dualising
map
\operatorname{PD}_{K}\colon\check{H}^{p}(K;A)\rightarrow H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K;A)
.

In the remainder of this section we will prove the following theorem.

Theorem A.20
.

(Poincaré Duality Theorem)

The map
\operatorname{PD}_{K}\colon\check{H}^{p}(K;A)\rightarrow H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K;A)
is a left
R
-module isomorphism for all compact subsets
K\subseteq M
.

Here, as above,
A
is an
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
-bimodule.
In the subsequent section we will see that
the Twisted Poincaré Duality Theorem
A.15
is a reasonably straightforward consequence of Theorem
A.20
.

The proof of Theorem
A.20
will be an application of the following lemma.

Lemma A.21
.

(Bootstrap lemma)

For each compact subspace
K\in M
let
P_{M}(K)
be a statement. If
P_{M}(\cdot)
satisfies the following three conditions:

(1)

P_{M}(K)
holds true for all compact subsets
K\subseteq M
with the property that for all
x\in K
the inclusions
\left\{x\right\}\to K
and
M\setminus K\to M\setminus\left\{x\right\}
are deformation retracts,

(2)

If
P_{M}(K_{1}),P_{M}(K_{2})
and
P_{M}(K_{1}\cap K_{2})
are true, then
P_{M}(K_{1}\cup K_{2})
is true,

(3)

If
\cdots\subseteq K_{2}\subseteq K_{1}
and
P_{M}(K_{i})
is true for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then
P_{M}(\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}K_{i})
is true.

Then
P_{M}(K)
is true for all
K\subseteq M
.

Proof.

See
[
Bre97
, Lemma VI.7.9]
.
∎

The idea is to apply the bootstrap lemma to the statement that
the conclusion of Theorem
A.20
holds for a given compact set
K
.
It turns out that condition (3) is the easiest to verify. It follows from formal properties about direct limits. For the verification of condition (1) we have to do one explicit calculation. This is the content of the next lemma.

Lemma A.22
.

Let
x\in M
be a point. The map
\operatorname{PD}_{\left\{x\right\}}\colon\check{H}^{0}(\left\{x\right\};A)\to H_{n}(M,M\setminus\left\{x\right\};A)
is an
R
-module isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
p\colon\widetilde{M}\rightarrow M
be the universal cover.
Since
x
is a point in a manifold we can calculate the dualising map
\operatorname{PD}_{\left\{x\right\}}
by taking the limit over open neighbourhoods
U
of
x
with the following two properties:

(1)

U
is contractible,

(2)

for any connected component
\overline{U}\subseteq p^{-1}(U)
the map
p|_{\overline{U}}
is a homeomorphism.

This can be done, since any neighbourhood of
x
contains a neighbourhood with these two properties. Let
U
be such a neighbourhood of
x
and
\overline{U}\subseteq p^{-1}(U)
a fixed connected component. This choice of connected component gives us an isomorphism
H^{0}(U;A)\cong A
as follows. Let
f\in H^{0}(U;A)
be arbitrary and
\overline{x}\in\overline{U}
be a point in our connected component. Then we get an element in
A
by evaluating
f([\overline{x}])
. Conversely, given an element
e\in A
we can construct a function in
H^{0}(U;A)
by setting
f([\overline{x}])=e
for all
\overline{x}\in\overline{U}
. Note that there is a unique way to extend
f
equivariantly to
C_{0}(p^{-1}(U);\mathbb{Z})
.

We are now going to construct a representative of the orientation class
\theta_{K}\in H_{n}(M,M\setminus\left\{x\right\};\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
for which it is very simple to calculate the dualising map.
Let
\overline{x}
be the preimage of
x
in
\overline{U}
. Now take a cycle
\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\sigma_{i}
which generates
H_{n}(\overline{U},\overline{U}\setminus\left\{\overline{x}\right\};\mathbb{Z})
. By Theorem
A.17
(2) and Lemma
A.19
one easily sees that
1\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\sigma_{i}
is a generator of
H_{n}(M,M\setminus\left\{x\right\};\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})
.

Using the isomorphism
H^{0}(U;A)\cong A
from above the dualising map becomes
\operatorname{PD}_{\left\{x\right\}}\colon A\to H_{n}(M,M\setminus\left\{x\right\};A),e\mapsto e\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\sigma_{i}
. This is clearly an isomorphism, since on the chain level we have:

C_{*}(U,U\setminus\left\{x\right\};A)=A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\pi}C_{*}(\gamma\overline{U},\gamma\overline{U}\setminus\left\{\gamma\overline{x}\right\};\mathbb{Z})\cong A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{*}(\overline{U},\overline{U}\setminus\left\{\overline{x}\right\};\mathbb{Z}).\qed

In order to verify condition (2) of the bootstrap lemma we will need the following lemma (compare
[
Bre97
, Lemma VI.8.2]
).

Lemma A.23
.

If
K
and
L
are two compact subsets of
M
, thenfor all
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\check{H}^{p}(K\!\cup\!L;A)\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{array}[]{c}\check{H}^{p}(K;A)\\
\oplus\\
\check{H}^{p}(L;A)\end{array}\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\check{H}^{p}(K\cap L;A)\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\check{H}^{p+1}(K\!\cup\!L;A)\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544ptH_{n-p}(M,M\!\setminus\!(K\!\cup\!L);A)\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\hskip-5.69046pt\begin{array}[]{c}H_{n-p}(M,M\!\setminus\!K;A)\\
\oplus\\
H_{n-p}(M,M\!\setminus\!L;A)\end{array}\hskip-5.69046pt\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544ptH_{n-p}(M,M\!\setminus\!(K\cap L);A)\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544ptH_{n-p-1}(M,M\!\setminus\!(K\!\cup\!L);A)\hskip-2.84544pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-2.84544pt\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}_{K\cup L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}_{K}\oplus\operatorname{PD}_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}_{K\cap L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}_{K\cup L}}

has exact rows and it commutes up to a sign depending only on
p
.

Proof.

The rows are exact by Mayer-Vietoris (see
Theorem
A.17
(3))
and the fact that direct limit is an exact functor. The commutativity of the squares is clear except for the last one involving the boundary map. This will be a painful diagram chase. Let
U\supseteq K
and
V\supseteq L
be open neighbourhoods containing
K
resp.
L
. The sequence in the top row comes from the short exact sequence (
\mathcal{U}=\left\{U,V\right\}
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}_{\mathcal{U}}(U\cup V;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(U;A)\oplus C^{*}(V;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(U\cap V;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

An element
\phi\in\check{H}^{p}(K\cap L;A)
will already be represented by some element
f\in C^{p}(U\cap V;A)
for some
U
and
V
as above.
We can extend
f
to an element
\overline{f}\in C^{p}(M;A)
by

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}f(\sigma)&\mbox{if }\operatorname{Im}\sigma\subseteq\widetilde{U}\cap\widetilde{V}\\
0&\mbox{else.}\end{cases}

Note that
\overline{f}\in C^{p}(M;A)
since
p^{-1}(U\cap V)
is an equivariant subspace and hence
\overline{f}
is equivariant.
If we consider
\overline{f}
as an element in
C^{p}(U;A)
then the cohomology class
\delta(\phi)
is represented by the cochain
h\in C^{p+1}(U\cup V;A)
which is given by

\displaystyle h(\sigma)=\begin{cases}\delta(\overline{f})(\sigma)&\mbox{if }\operatorname{Im}\sigma\subseteq\widetilde{U}\\
0&\mbox{else.}\end{cases}

Since
\phi
is a cocycle we have
\delta(f)(\sigma)=0
for
\sigma\in C_{*}(U\cap V;A)
. It follows in particular that if
\sigma
is a simplex whose image is completely contained in
\widetilde{V}
, then
h(\sigma)=0
.
We can represent our orientation class
\theta\in H_{n}(M,M\setminus(K\cup L))
by a cycle

\displaystyle a=b+c+d+e\quad\mbox{with}\quad

\displaystyle b\in C_{n}(U\cap V;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})\quad c\in C_{n}(U\setminus(U\cap L);\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})

\displaystyle d\in C_{n}(V\setminus(V\cap K);\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}}),\quad e\in C_{n}(M\setminus(K\cup L);\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}}).

Obviously
e
does not play a role since we kill it in the end. With these representatives one computes that

\delta(\phi)(\theta)
is represented by

h\acap(b+c+d)=\delta(\overline{f})\cap c+h\acap d+\delta(f)\cap b=\delta(\overline{f})\cap c.

The pairing of
h
with
b
is zero since
f
was a cocycle in
C^{*}(U\cap V;A)
and the pairing of
h
with
d
is zero since
d
consists of simplices with image in
\widetilde{V}
.

The lower sequence comes from the short exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(M,M\setminus(K\cup L);A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}C_{*}(M,M\setminus K;A)\\
\oplus\\
C_{*}(M,M\setminus L);A)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(M,M\setminus(K\cap L);A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Before we compute the other side
\partial(\phi\acap\ex_{U\cap V}(\theta))
we want to recall that the cap product is natural on the chain complex level i.e. the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{p}(U;A)\times C_{n}(U,U\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(U,U\setminus K;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{p}(M;A)\times C_{n}(M,M\setminus K;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(M,M\setminus K;A).}

Therefore we use the representatives from above. To construct the boundary map
\partial
, we take as the preimage of
\overline{f}\acap a\in C_{*}(M,M\setminus(K\cap L);A)
the element
(\overline{f}\acap a,0)\in C_{*}(M,M\setminus K;A)\oplus C_{*}(M,M\setminus L;A)
. Then one computes in
C_{*}(M,M\setminus K;A)

\displaystyle\partial(\overline{f}\acap a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\cdot\delta(\overline{f})\acap a\pm f\acap\partial a\quad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lem:capboundary}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\cdot\delta(\overline{f})\acap a\quad(\mbox{since $f\acap\partial a\in C_{n-p-1}(M\setminus(K\cup L);A)$})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\cdot\delta(\overline{f})\acap b+c+d+e

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\cdot\delta(\overline{f})\acap(c+d)\quad\mbox{(same reason as above)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\cdot\delta(\overline{f})\acap c\quad\mbox{(since $d\in C_{n-p}(V\setminus(K\cap V);A)$)}

Therefore the element
\partial(\phi\acap\ex_{U\cap V}(\theta))
is also represented by
(-1)^{p+1}\cdot\delta(\overline{f})\acap c\in C_{n-p-1}(M,M\setminus(K\cup L);A)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A.20
.

Let
P_{M}(K)
be the statement that the map
\operatorname{PD}_{K}
is an isomorphism.
Then it is sufficient to verify condition (1), (2) and (3) of the bootstrap lemma.
We start by verifying (1). In the case that
K=\left\{x\right\}
is just a point we have already seen in Lemma
A.22
that the statement holds true.
For a general compact
K
with the property of (1) the statement follows from the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{p}(K;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{p}(\left\{x\right\};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus\left\{x\right\};A),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the vertical maps are isomorphisms by the homotopy invariance and the bottom row by the observation above. Hence condition (1) is verified.

Condition (2) follows immediately from the five lemma and Lemma
A.23
.

Let
K_{i}
be a sequence of compact subsets such that
P_{M}(K_{i})
holds for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We set
K=\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}K_{i}
. It is an exercise in point set topology of manifolds that each
K_{i}
has a fundamental system
U_{i,j}
of open neighbourhoods. Fundamental system means that
U_{i,j}\subseteq U_{i,k}
if
j<k
and that for each open set
U
containing
K_{i}
there is a
j
such that
U_{i,j}\subseteq U
.
Another exercise in the point set topology of manifolds shows that one can construct these sets such that
U_{1,j}\supseteq U_{2,j}\supseteq U_{3,j}\supseteq\ldots
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
U_{i,j}
is a fundamental system of open neighbourhoods of
K
with the order
(i,j)\leq(k,l)\Leftrightarrow i\leq k\ \wedge j\leq l
.
One has the natural isomorphism
[
Bre97
, Appendix D5]
:

\varinjlim_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\check{H}^{p}(K_{i};A)=\varinjlim_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\varinjlim_{j\in\mathbb{N}}H^{p}(U_{i,j};A)\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\varinjlim_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}H^{p}(U_{i,j};A)\cong\check{H}^{p}(K;A).

Hence the theorem follows from the commutativity of the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varinjlim}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\check{H}^{p}(K_{i};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varinjlim}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K_{i};A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{p}(K;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K;A).}

∎

A.5.
Proof of the Twisted Poincaré Duality Theorem

For the reader’s convenience we recall the main theorem from the last section. Here, as above,
R
is a ring and
A
is an
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
-bimodule.

Theorem
A.20
.

Let
M
be a compact, oriented, connected
n
-dimensional manifold.
The map
\operatorname{PD}_{K}\colon\check{H}^{p}(K;A)\rightarrow H_{n-p}(M,M\setminus K;A)
is an isomorphism of left
R
-modules for all compact subsets
K\subseteq M
and al
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Furthermore, we also recall that we need to prove the following theorem.

Theorem
A.15
.

Let
M
a compact, oriented, connected
n
-dimensional manifold. Let
S
and
T
be codimension 0 compact submanifolds of
\partial M
such that
\partial S=\partial T=S\cap T
and
\partial M=S\cup T
.
Let
[M]\in H_{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
be the fundamental class of
M
. The map

-\acap[M]\colon H^{k}(M,S;{A})\,\,\rightarrow\,\,H_{n-k}(M,T;A)

defined by Lemma
A.13
is an isomorphism of left
R
-modules.

In the remainder of this appendix we will explain how to deduce
Theorem
A.15
from Theorem
A.20
.
First note that if
M
is a closed manifold, then we can set

K=M
in Theorem
A.20
.
Evidently we have
\check{H}^{p}(M;A)=H(M;A)
.
Thus we obtain precisely the statement of Theorem
A.15
in the closed case.

Next let
M
be a compact oriented manifold with nonempty boundary.
First we consider the case
R=\varnothing
and
S=\partial M
.
By the Collar Neighbourhood Theorem
2.16
there exists a collar
\partial M\times[0,2]\subseteq M
of the boundary such that
\partial M=\partial M\times\left\{0\right\}
. We obtain the following chain of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle H^{p}(M;A)

\displaystyle\cong H^{p}(M\setminus(\partial M\times[0,1));A)\quad\mbox{(homotopy)}

\displaystyle\cong\check{H}^{p}(M\setminus(\partial M\times[0,1));A)\quad\mbox{(follows from considering the open}

neighbourhoods
M\setminus(\partial M\times[0,1-\frac{1}{n}])
)

\displaystyle\cong H_{n-p}(M\setminus\partial M,\partial M\times(0,1);A)\quad\mbox{(duality $K=M\setminus(\partial M\times[0,1)))$}

\displaystyle\cong H_{n-p}(M,\partial M\times[0,1);A)\quad\mbox{(excision $U=\partial M$)}

\displaystyle\cong H_{n-p}(M,\partial M;A),

It follows from the definition of the dualising map and naturality of cap product that these isomorphisms are given by capping with a generator
[M]\in H_{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{triv}})\cong H_{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
as in the classical case.

The proof of the general case of Theorem
A.15
relies on the following lemma.

Lemma A.24
.

Let
M
be a compact, oriented, connected
n
-dimensional manifold. Let
R
and
S
be compact codimension 0 submanifolds of
\partial M
such that
\partial R=\partial S=R\cap S
and
\partial M=R\cup S
. For each
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the following diagram commutes up to a sign:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p}(M,\partial M;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p}(M,R;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p}(\partial M,R;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{p+1}(M,\partial M;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p-1}(S,\partial S;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p}(M;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p}(M,S;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p-1}(S;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-p-1}(M;A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\acap{[M]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\acap{\left[M\right]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\emph{Theorem}}~\ref{thm:basicproperties}(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\acap{[M]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\acap{\left[R\right]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

Proof.

The commutativity is a more or less direct consequence of Lemma
A.13
and the observation that
\partial_{*}[M]=[\partial M]
. More precisely, the proof in the untwisted case is given in detail in
[
Fri23
, p. 2892]
. The proof in the twisted case is basically the same.
∎

The proof of the general case of Theorem
A.15
follows from
the previously discussed Poincaré Duality isomorphisms

\acap[M]\colon H^{p}(M;A)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-p}(M,\partial M;A)
,

\acap[R]\colon H^{p+1}(R,A)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-p-1}(R,\partial R;A)

together with Lemma
A.24
and the five lemma.
